The Wrong Kind of American
2026-08-17

The Wrong Kind of American

The Atlantic | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
At the CIA, they’d started an office pool on when I’d be fired. President Trump would be sworn in for his second term at noon; the smart money had me gone by 12:15. But the smart money hadn’t counted on Trump’s first pick to run intelligence for the National Security Council being shredded by an online mob on suspicion of disloyalty. There wasn’t yet anyone in place to fire me. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. After taking the oath, Trump spoke at the Capitol. “As of today, it will henceforth be the official policy of the United States government that there are only two genders: male and female.” “Did he … ?” an NSA colleague asked. She glanced at me and looked away. “Yeah,” I muttered. The good news: I was one of those two genders. The bad news: The administration would have views as to which one. I gripped my badge. I could take it off, leave the White House complex, walk out the north gate, and never look back. I imagined the story they would tell: See? Even she knew she didn’t belong. I didn’t move. I’d wanted this badge since I was a kid. I’d chosen to be here. “I know you all heard what the president said,” I told my team after Trump’s speech. “There’s a rumor of an executive order on bathrooms. If anyone asks, I’m using the coffee shops across the street.” My whole team spoke up: Julia, we’ve got your back. “Me being trans is not a secret,” I reminded them. “Promise me: Nobody covers for me.” Back in my office, I closed the door. I cried quickly, efficiently, no sound—­the way you do when the walls have ears, and everyone thinks you’re fearless. Joe Biden’s political appointees had left the week before, and I was now the senior intelligence official in the White House. For the incoming Trump administration, the trans woman would man the gates. For as long as I can remember, I’ve carried two dreams. The first came true in 2007, when I raised my hand, standing on the agency’s marble seal, and became a CIA officer. The second dream, I didn’t dare speak aloud; it came from ­an unexplainable conviction that I should have been born a woman. The longing, like homesickness, grew into an unbearable darkness. On a sweltering June morning in 2013, I sat in the CIA parking lot, hands clamped on the wheel, preparing myself to live out my second dream. Terrified of my first day at work as Julia, I’d chosen a blue button-down and pants, hoping no one would notice I’d changed genders. At the turnstile, the guard smiled. “Morning, ma’am,” he said, and the badge reader flashed green. To use the lexicon of my trade, the after-action review would have read: “Nothing significant to report.” In the years ahead—­traveling to 35 countries, briefing former President George W. Bush, becoming a mom—­being trans would sometimes seem like the most ordinary part of my life. But as much as I wanted to disappear into normal womanhood and the work, I knew there were others who needed to see that a life like mine was possible. So, in 2017, I wrote in these pages under the pseudonym Jenny Hall about how being openly trans at the CIA made me a better officer. CIA recruiters used the essay on college campuses, and new officers—­not realizing they were talking with the author—told me that it had inspired them to apply. Read: Coming out as transgender made me a more effective CIA officer Soon after the essay ran, a call went out for volunteers to join the President’s Daily Brief staff and serve the new Trump administration. Working the PDB meant staying up all night to distill the most sensitive intelligence we possessed into a book that the president and our senior leaders read at dawn. It was one of the CIA’s most coveted assignments—a proving ground for our best analysts. But the new administration was led by a president who had praised Vladimir Putin and compared the intelligence community to the Nazis. Almost nobody volunteered. I did. Growing up in Hawaii, I dreamed of seeing the world. My grandfather gave me a simple promise: “Work hard, don’t complain; the country will take care of you. We came to America for small things. No pogroms. No leaky roof. Paradise was a bonus.” Service before politics. Country before self. I clung to this promise as I became a man, a husband, a father, a CIA officer, a wife, a mother. For two years I served the Trump White House, arriving at work around midnight, six days a week, roaming the West Wing before dawn. My wife learned to live with the silence at the dinner table. Sometimes I filled in on the road—­summers in Bedminster, winters in Palm Beach. On my first trip, I boarded at Joint Base Andrews and a card waited at my seat: Ms. Curlee, Welcome Aboard Air Force One. Grateful for the welcome, I was less grateful to whoever had sat me in the middle of the White House press pool. So I stuffed my face with the best waffles of my life and kept small talk to a minimum. As we flew to Mar-a-Lago, the president’s aide beckoned me to the front of the plane. No one cared that a trans woman was briefing President Trump. Then one morning, my boss walked in, a wide smile on her face. She had a new job for me. “Julia, what would you think about briefing the vice president?” Days later, I stood in his driveway at the Naval Observatory. I must be insane, I thought. Mike Pence had built his career on public opposition to marriages like mine—­to people like me. But for all our differences, he was the vice president. He needed the best intel support possible, and this was my country too. “Mr. Vice President,” I said, “it’s an honor to meet you. My name is Julia”—like I’d practiced a thousand times. “You’re abandoning me with her?” Pence teased his outgoing briefer, with a line I’d heard he used with other people. He turned to me. “Okay, Julia, tell me something I don’t know.” “Sir, did you know that a third of Purdue’s computer-science grad students are Chinese nationals?” I grabbed my case and climbed into the Beast, the nickname for the executive limo. I hunted in vain for a seat belt as Secret Service radios squawked, “On the move.” I dug in my heels as the floor pitched. Pennsylvania Avenue, the PDB, and me—­alone with Mike Pence. When I got home, my wife and I decided to keep my new boss’s identity a secret. In my community, his name was shorthand for everything we feared. I could imagine the optics, and God help us if someone called a reporter. For the next year, I briefed him nearly every morning. As soon as the coffee was hot, Pence was ready to work. He ripped through the briefing book, firing questions at me, scribbling in the margins. Down the hall, the president was using his phone to light up Twitter. The vice president preferred to use our multibillion-­dollar intel apparatus to save lives. Some mornings he would push back on an assessment, but I would hold the line, explaining how the conclusions had been reached. That was the job. When my tour was almost up, he asked me to extend it. One morning, as we approached the White House gates, the vice president turned to me. “Julia, do you know why they call it ‘the People’s House’?” “Right up until the Civil War, anyone could come to the door and wait to see the president.” As the steel gates opened, he leaned in. “You know, it’s a privilege to work here.” When my tour finally ended, Pence invited my family to the White House. In his office, he took my mother’s hand and smiled: “You must be very proud of your daughter.” Years before, my mother had told me that in her grief, she’d felt that—­somehow—I’d killed her son. Now I’d done the impossible: lived both dreams, becoming an intelligence officer and a woman. Now the vice president was holding my mother’s hand. No one has affirmed my gender identity quite like Mike Pence. On my last day, I gave him a letter. I wrote: “I’m a trans­gender CIA officer, a wife and mother—­and I served the Vice President. Anything is possible in America.” I told him many people at the CIA hadn’t believed that a trans officer like me could serve someone like him. “Well,” he said, “I’m glad I passed the test.” In that moment, it felt like the battle had been won. For years, friends told me people need to know that a gay trans woman had worked for Mike Pence and that the world hadn’t exploded. No—I was adamant—we serve in silence. But I kept the photos of that time. After January 6, 2021, the vice president invited his former PDB briefers for a farewell picture in the West Wing. The place was quieter than I’d ever heard it. As we said our goodbyes, my voice shook. “Sir,” I said, “what you did last week—­I’ve never been prouder to have worked for you.” He nodded. “Thank you,” he said. “That means a lot to me.” We shook hands. The mob at the Capitol had chanted for his hanging. He’d done his duty to the country, certifying the vote. I’d done mine to him. That was the bargain. Service before politics. Country before self. Read: Transgender soldiers want the dignity of serving their country Two and a half years later, after a stint back at the CIA, I returned to the White House as a director for intelligence programs on President Biden’s National Security Council. Days after Trump’s 2024 election victory, the intelligence staff gathered in our boss’s office. He was not a man who frightened easily. But he knew what was coming. “I need to strongly advise you to leave before Inauguration Day,” he said. “There’s a world in which you’ll be targeted individually.” He didn’t say my name. He didn’t need to. “They know how to use the tools this time. Install loyalists at CIA, FBI, and Justice—turn clearances and investigations into weapons.” I told him I wasn’t resigning. Then the CIA’s deputy director sat me down. “Julia, look at me. You don’t have to do this. You have a target on your back that others don’t.” He searched my face, looking for a crack. When he didn’t find one, he nodded. “Good luck.” Days before the inauguration, Biden gathered the staff in the East Wing. “Now, folks, here’s the deal. Trump doesn’t know it yet, but he needs you. It’s important for the country.” Of all the senior government officials who spoke with us before the new administration took office, Biden was the only one who asked us to stay. On Inauguration Day, I stood in the West Wing to brief the new national security adviser, minutes after he’d taken office. Down the hall, the president signed an executive order banning me from restrooms in all federal buildings. I would start to plan policy meetings around bathroom stops across Pennsylvania Avenue. The next day, my team fanned out to brief the new leadership on our nation’s highest secrets. Then these leaders stripped my insurance coverage for the estrogen that keeps me healthy, voided my discrimination protections, and declared me “male” to foreign governments on my passport. I would keep briefing them anyway. On the third day came the purge—160 career NSC staff members sent home. Then new White House officials asked me to read them into classified programs; they called me around the clock, desperate for access to intelligence. Programs crucial to U.S. interests sat unmanaged; ­the officers who had been in charge of them had been escorted out. When NSC leaders realized what they’d done, it fell to me to call the people they’d humiliated and beg them to come back. They did. One week in, the administration I served reinstated its ban on trans people in the military. Decorated officers such as my friend Colonel Bree Fram were thrown out for the crime of being honest. In the White House, people called me “in­dispensable.” I still was not welcome in their bathrooms. Adam Serwer: The attack on trans rights won’t end there A month later, Director of National Intelligence Tulsi Gabbard fired 100 intelligence officers, many of them trans, for taking part in an LGBTQ group chat that conservative media had cast as sexually explicit. The next day, I ran an intel briefing—­for her—­because that is what we do. The following week, the president stood before Congress and called trans children a lie. Then, one Saturday, I was sitting in a rib joint in Roanoke, Virginia, when my phone buzzed. “Julia, sorry to bother,” my boss said. “I have bad news. Your White House tour’s over. Chief of staff called. I can’t give you a reason.” I was elbows-deep in brisket, the sauce congealing. I’d beaten the “Fire Julia” office pool by about 68 days. I was being sent back to my home agency. My boss cleared his throat. “Um, Julia—we still need you Monday. But please. Be discreet.” Fired on Saturday. Needed on Monday. But stay invisible? Then my phone exploded—a dozen friends forwarding the same tweet. “SCOOP: A transgender, Biden holdover who hates President Trump is still working in the NSC intel office,” the right-wing provocateur Laura Loomer had written. “If you are aware of this person and have their name, please send it to me and I will post their identity.” Monday arrived, and cherry blossoms framed a perfect spring day in Washington. I reported to the White House. I had no badge, and my accounts had been revoked. I spent three marathon days helping the administration that had fired me finish the budget, and handing off the portfolio I’d overseen for two years. As I packed up, a string of senior Trump appointees stopped in to say goodbye. One took my hand. “Thank you for staying as long as you did,” he whispered. “Not many would have.” That night, a list of hidden “enemies” in the administration provided by Loomer landed on the president’s desk. But an internet activist like Loomer doesn’t come up with names on her own, or just walk into the Oval to give them to the president. Someone in the building had helped her. Most of those fired, including my boss, were rock-ribbed Republicans, experts in their fields, who had been courteous and professional. As I walked out the north gate with my small box of belongings, I finally understood the warning: “Be discreet.” The White House had needed me. Trump officials had known I was trans from day one. This hadn’t been an issue until it was public—­and then nothing else I’d done mattered. Not my service, not my war-zone tours, not even which restroom I used. One morning after returning to the CIA, I passed a tour group in our museum, clustered below a photo of me high on the wall, near one of the 33rd president. “Here next to President Truman,” the guide said, “is a PDB briefer on Air Force One. Our highest secrets, collected at vast expense and great risk.” “How do you become a briefer?” someone asked. The guide smiled. “Very competitive. Years of experience. Our best.” The tour moved on to the Osama bin Laden raid. I stayed. My eyes drifted over the photo, from my face in black and white to the vice president’s PDB in my lap. I’d spent decades fighting for my place—from a beach boy who dreamed of seeing the world to an officer who served her country. Now my photo hung as an artifact of a time when the CIA proudly said the quality of the work was more important than the gender of the person doing it. My eyes fell to the door below—the door to the restroom I was barred from entering. Every year since my first day as Julia, I’d sent a Pride Month email to my colleagues. But by June 2025, they’d banned the CIA’s LGBTQ group. Pride flags that had flown for decades were suddenly gone. My finger hovered over the “Send” button. Two sentences: I froze. Someone would report this email to drive me out of the agency, just like at the White House. Don’t end up the last openly trans CIA officer, I told myself. I deleted the email, breathing through the urge to do something heroic and stupid. In the screen’s dark reflection, I saw my face—exhausted, angry. That was the moment I knew I had to resign. I’d weighed my family’s health insurance against two sentences of solidarity and chosen to surrender my integrity. The agency that had taught me to tell hard truths was now teaching me to be invisible. It was time to go. I loved the agency. I loved its mission. I love it still. I served under four presidents and through three wars. I lived by my grandfather’s words: Work hard, don’t complain; the country will take care of you. In 2017, I closed my Atlantic essay with the words “And trans kids should know, CIA is always recruiting.” That promise has been broken. I cannot in good conscience advise LGBTQ Americans to join the CIA—and that sentence is the hardest thing I have ever written. The agency has been gutted as part of a deliberate plan, the director of the Office of Management and Budget once boasted, to put the people who defend our country “in trauma.” Analysts have been fired in public or questioned by the FBI; decade-old assessments have been denounced by the CIA director in the press. The president calls analysis “virtual treason” when it contradicts his preferred reality, and uses the CIA to undermine public confidence in American elections. Fear has done its work. Irreplaceable officers with crucial language and technical skills, and decades of experience, have walked out the door. Those who remain within an agency built to deliver hard truths are being muzzled. Twenty years ago, I sat in a bunker in Iraq, praying I would survive the night, in a war begun after our intelligence community had failed to do enough to challenge what the White House wanted to hear. Our agency spent the next two decades repairing the trust that failure broke. I know institutions can be rebuilt. Officers can come back, but only if someone is willing to tell the truth. In the entrance of our Original Headquarters Building, across from the stars for every officer slain in the line of duty, you can still read the words carved in marble: And ye shall know the truth and the truth shall make you free. I know that the CIA friends I left behind are holding the line under im­possible circumstances. The country needs them more than ever. And I understand my colleagues who left but can’t speak up. Their livelihoods depend on clearances and contracts that can be revoked with a phone call. But we all swore an oath to the Constitution. I’m afraid too. This administration has already taken most of what I had to lose. I know that the White House and its online mob will come for me. My ancestors fled pogroms; they knew a fear I never expected to feel in the United States. I was offered an untenable bargain: Don’t make trouble. Take the insults—­deviant, diversity hire, man in a dress—­from those who have never faced enemy fire, who asked for my assistance in private and then signed the orders to erase people like me because “transgender ideology” was a threat to Western civilization. Flailing regimes dehumanize minorities to distract from their failures. When the economy collapses or a war goes badly, they find a tiny group of people, make them the enemy within, and rally the country against them. This is how it’s gone in Iran. Hungary. Russia. I wrote PDBs about it. This will not stop with trans people. It never has. But that caricature works only if people like me don’t exist—and I exist. I speak now because somewhere, a trans kid is watching. I want that kid to know that they are not alone. I was a trans kid too, and I’ll be damned if I let another generation grow up the way I did—told that they don’t belong, that they don’t deserve a job, a bathroom, a future. If I could brief presidents and serve in the White House—twice—so can they. I had planned to spend the rest of my career serving my country at the CIA. This White House took my calling, but it will not take my pride, or tell me that I don’t belong, or that I must disappear. I once wrote under a pseudonym so I could keep serving. I write now in my own name for the same reason. This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “The Wrong Kind of American.”
The Atlantic
Why Learn Something That a Machine Can Do for You?
2026-08-17

Why Learn Something That a Machine Can Do for You?

The Atlantic | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube | Pocket Casts Children still learn long division even though calculators exist, and many people continue to study languages even as AI translation becomes faster and more accurate. Is there a deeper purpose for learning that technology will never touch? In this episode of How to Touch Grass, the co-hosts Natalie Brennan and Julie Beck hear from Michelle Miller, a cognitive psychologist at Northern Arizona University who studies technology and learning. She explains that although the changing tides of tech may shape what and how we learn, as humans we still have the autonomy to build the minds we want. The following is a transcript of the episode: Michelle Miller: Learning is how we build ourselves as individuals, and it’s how humanity shapes its course. Julie Beck: Michelle Miller has thought a lot about the purpose of learning. She’s a cognitive psychologist at Northern Arizona University focused on attention and memory and how technology shapes education. As she said, learning is a very human endeavor. But what we learn and the kinds of learning we value are shaped by the changing tides of technology. Beck: I’m Julie Beck, a staff writer at The Atlantic. Natalie Brennan: And I’m Natalie Brennan, a producer at The Atlantic. Brennan: Julie, that sentiment—that what we learn changes with the tides of technology—feels really relevant to a change I’ve noticed in the last decade. I think of coding and how big of a push that was when I was in college to Learn to code or you’ll be left behind. But just a few years later, now my coder friends are really worried about job security, and the thing we’re being pressed to learn is learning how to correctly prompt AI so that it can code. Beck: Mmm. Yeah, I’ve noticed that too. And the thing that I keep thinking about now that so many people are starting to outsource so many things to AI—coding and other things—one of the biggest questions I keep coming back to is: What is the point of learning something when you no longer have to? When a machine could just do it for you? Brennan: Well, this is one of those questions that keeps coming back around, right? Why do I need to learn math if a calculator can do it? Beck: And yet, generations of children still continue to learn math! Beck: So we’ve come to some consensus that there is value to learning long division even if you don’t end up using it much as an adult. But the question feels especially relevant right now, because the kind of things that technology is currently threatening to replace are more wide-ranging and complex than just arithmetic. Miller: I look back and I say, At every big turning point in history, how many times was it: and then we learned how to? This is what forward motion really is. Beck: We really wanted to talk to Michelle about this because she has a really expansive view of what technology is and how it shapes us. Not just whether tech is good or bad for learning, but what kinds of tech help or hurt, under what circumstances. Brennan: And it all depends on how we use that technology. Michelle, I’m curious: Have you learned anything new recently? Miller: Oh my gosh. Well, one of the things that I’m working on right now is barebow archery. Miller: So really low-tech, you know, think really traditional wooden bow and so on. And I have a coach, and I share my archery range with a lot of individuals who are doing more of the actual hunting type of archery with the big compound bows. So that’s not me. I’m the lady who’s over shooting at 15 yards just with my little quiver of arrows trying to just get a little bit better every single time. Beck: I was gonna ask if, you know, technology was involved in anything that you had learned recently, but I mean, I guess a bow is technology of a very ancient sort. Miller: It absolutely is. And it is funny, though, that even something that is so almost primal in that very physical, what we think of as low-tech, although it would’ve been high-tech at some point in human history— Beck: In the Bronze Age or whenever that was, yeah. Miller: —it’s still complemented these days by technology. And I think of something else that—I went through a relearning journey about starting 20 years ago, which is knitting. Miller: But it is fascinating that there, too, you think of something that’s so tactile, so physical, and it’s really enjoyed a resurgence in the modern age because of technology. Because we have the ability to, you know, I no longer have to depend on maybe my great aunt or the lady down the street to show me, Oh my gosh, how to get out of this problem? I have my choice of instructional videos. There’s communities. There’s whole sites that are just devoted to this. So— Beck: That’s definitely true when it comes to my hobbies. But I want to step way back for a sec, for the big philosophical view. Are you ready? Beck: What, to you, is the ultimate purpose of learning? Miller: Ah, the ultimate purpose of learning. I think that there’s a lot of ways that we as people and our minds and our brains evolve and change over the course of our lifetimes, and some of those are kind of out of our control. They’re sort of “nature takes its course.” But the changes that are wrought through learning are the ones that, in a sense, we have the most agency and control over, that we can self-direct. Whether it’s saying, I wanna learn a new crochet stitch, and this would be a way to make something that’s unique or to spend time with people I care about, or to test myself and my own creativity and limits. The way that we can do that is learning. And we’ve talked about the technology angle of it, but it’s still also something really social. I think that teaching another human being how to do something—that is one of the highest and best expressions of humanity that there is. It’s one of the best and most positive ways that we interact with and contribute to one another. So I do think it comes back to that core, our self-agency and our connection to this great web of humanity. I mean, it comes about through learning. Beck: Yeah. Oh my gosh. Okay, it seems like we’re kind of coming away with: The point of learning is that you are able to kind of direct your own experience in this life in a way that you might otherwise, in a lot of ways, just be kinda tossed about on the tides of, like, history and aging. Is that fair to say? Miller: I think that’s fair to say. And, I mean, to maybe even elevate it a little bit more, especially as a kind of an introverted person myself, I think of my own mind is that’s where I live. That’s ultimately, well, literally at the end of the day, that’s where we’re at. And our mind is our experience, and learning builds the mind that we live in. Beck: Yes. I mean, ultimately, we are all just, like, in the black box of our own skulls, unfortunately, when it comes to how we engage with the world. We can’t really get out of there. When do you think technology helps with that purpose, and when does it get in the way? Miller: So I like how you put that question of not good or bad, but more when. And I would add to that how. So instructional designers will always say, You have to start with the end in mind. So, you know, a professor will walk in and say, Well, I heard about this technology or this application, this quiz game or whatever at a conference. Should I do it? And they will always take a breath and say, What are you trying to accomplish? Or sometimes, What problem are you trying to solve? Where do you know that students are going to struggle? That’s where, sometimes, the boost of a technology tool can be helpful. And one case in point that really illustrates this, I think pretty clearly, is this: So there’s this principle or phenomenon called “retrieval practice.” We often contrast it with, for many students, good-faith effort. They think the way to study, say, a list of vocabulary terms, they think, Okay, I’m gonna reread that part of my textbook over and over. I’m gonna highlight, and I’m gonna read it again, and I’m gonna read it again. I’m gonna kind of have a general little gut check and say, ‘Uh, did that feel familiar? Oh, yeah, yeah, I’ve got it.’ But what will deliver the ability to retain that stuff you’re reviewing is to close the book and answer questions, even if you don’t know the material very well. It just produces these huge, long-lasting effects that are very, very versatile. Miller: Yeah. So how does that figure in with technology? Well, first it was CD-ROM databases that had lots of quiz questions that students could answer, and you could track how the whole class was doing, and students could get immediate feedback. They didn’t have to wait for me to grade. It was great. And then we moved on to things like these little in-class clickers, where the whole class could do retrieval practice and answer a question. And now with AI, AI can generate lots of really high-quality questions, even open-ended questions. We’re no longer locked into multiple choice. So that’s a great example of how—it’s not just like, well, grab a phone, open an app, learning happens. But here’s a principle that helps accelerate this one particular aspect of learning. An expert has gone through and selected and said, Well, here’s what you need to know. And so we marry those two together, and then we get something that actually does deliver something that’s positive for students. Miller: Where it detracts. If there’s something that you want to experience and remember in the moment, that's a different form of memory. That’s what we call episodic memory. So if I’m finally visiting, say, in my area, the Grand Canyon, and I want to see this and remember this moment, that’s where technology is not necessarily going to help. Even things like—it’s very ironic: We take pictures to remember an experience, but there’s research showing that we remember less from an experience when we’re walking around taking pictures of it. And it’s really just an attention effect. Just these little phones—even if you have it on “Do not disturb,” setting up the shot takes away, takes you out of that moment. Miller: So when that is the concern, technology is not your friend. Beck: Hmm. That’s so interesting. As you were saying that, I was thinking about, you know, people— whenever you go to a concert these days, right, everybody just has their phone up and they’re recording the video of their favorite song or they’re taking a picture. And I hadn’t thought about it as learning, but I guess in a way it’s sort of like, your memory of your life is like learning the story of your life. So maybe you are not retaining that concert so much. Miller: Perfect example. Yeah, we don’t have different, you know, pieces of the brain or parts of the memory that are like, Okay, here’s learning; here’s serious learning, and here’s all the other stuff. Beck: I guess to drill into it a little more, what is specifically the value of learning a thing that you can just easily outsource to technology and you don’t ever need to do it? Like, I would like to remember my trip to the Grand Canyon, like sure, but I don’t ever need to calculate a tip ever again in my whole life. I’ll just do it on my phone. Miller: Yeah. And I mean, if that’s really the case, then I say: Have at it; pull out the phone. Beck: Mm. But is there still, I don’t know, whether it’s philosophical, spiritual, cognitive value of learning a thing that you don’t have to learn? For instance, learning a foreign language when you can just translate it with AI, or learning how to code when, from all reports, it seems like AI is kinda taking over that industry. Is there still value to learning those things regardless of that progress? Miller: Yeah, and as I’m kinda standing—I think of myself kind of standing at the bottom of a mountain of: I wanna become expert in. And let’s take, for example, I wanna be an expert in fashion design. You know, where does that step start? It starts with really getting into the nuts and the bolts of the construction of what you’re doing. So maybe, if that’s my goal, I wanna learn how to knit a sweater from hand or sew a pair of pants. And even as an expert fashion designer, how many times am I gonna just sit down and sew a garment for a client or just knit something by hand? Beck: Probably send it to the factory, right? Miller: Yeah, you send it to the factory! But to really understand How does fabric behave? Why do I wanna make cable stitches in a particular way? And how is cashmere different than cotton when I work with it? I wonder if there really is no substitute for sitting with it. So it’s not—I don’t wanna make it sound like, Oh, you gotta take your medicine and pay your dues. I don’t think it has to be like that. I think it’s more in the spirit of exploration and the spirit of actually physically seeing: How do the building blocks that I’m gonna work with from here on out, how do those behave? So that’s an argument for learning from the ground up, even when we can look it up. Beck: Okay, this is gonna sound very stupid, but why is or how is AI different than a calculator? Miller: Okay. AI and calculator. Oh my gosh, talk about a conversational football that we’ve been throwing back and forth in my field right now. I mean, at its core, it does offer a few ways to do things that were time-consuming and maybe even sometimes do them more accurately. Well, that’s where a calculator perhaps is even better than AI in that way. Calculators don’t hallucinate. They are one-trick ponies, but boy, they do that trick really, really well. So that’s one perhaps smaller difference. But AI is not like a calculator in that it can mimic authentic human communication. In, say, contemporary higher education, you hear horror stories of students who are like—you ask them an icebreaker question like “What’s your favorite movie?” and they have AI pick a favorite movie and fill that out. And you’re just going, Oh, why? And here I am on the other end, pouring my heart and soul, and not AI, into this course. And who’s even on the other end? So I think it raises more questions about what’s kind of the contract between professor and teacher and student? Beck: Yeah. Well, thinking about the calculator comparison, I mean, that’s something that I just hear a lot from people who are, you know, very excited about AI or not too concerned. Just sort of saying, Calculators made us more efficient. They let us just do things better. And we don’t generally consider using a calculator to be morally bad or a sign that we are degrading our ability to think because we did our tip calculation on a calculator. But does AI fall into that similar category to you, where it’s like, Ah, this is just progress. We can outsource stuff we don’t need to do, or is something about it inherently different? Miller: Well, I do think that it challenges in a new way, in a perhaps a more painful way, our ego, frankly, and our value as educators, and that’s something that I also encourage people to think about. How much of this resistance, if you have it, is because you’re saying, Well the knowledge that I have for you, what I had prepared, what I want to profess, has a different value now than it once did. And I don’t know, but I would wager that there were people back in the day, individuals, who could add, subtract, and divide. That was a high-end skill. And somebody came along with an abacus, and now any old person can do it. Or being able to use a slide rule like my grandfather could do as an engineer. So I kinda keep falling back on those as well. But I think the upside is also potentially so much more vast than a simpler, more targeted one-trick pony like a calculator. Beck: Hmm. The way that I’ve been thinking about it is like, are you outsourcing a task? Which could be good or bad or neutral, depending on the situation. Or are you outsourcing your own thought process? Which to me is like a betrayal of your own humanity. Is that a reasonable way to think about it or would you frame it differently? Miller: Mm-hmm. I mean, if I would reframe it at all, kind of the way you’re coming at this, it’s also to me a matter of who’s in control of it? I think that we’re starting to, those of us who’ve tried AI, we’ve started to have that feeling of like: When is it driving the conversation and just doing the thing? And when am I giving instructions? I’ve even played around with— I wish that we could say, instead of, “Oh, ask AI,” “Tell AI.” Wouldn’t that be a different world? Because that’s where AI shines, is when it does exactly what you, the human user, tells it to do. So I would love for us to kind of charge into this, um, with that sense of, I am the boss of you. I am giving instructions. I am telling you. Don’t do—I mean, we learn pretty quickly. That’s one of the most useful phrases that you can share in something like a ChatGPT is, “Do not write or draft or change until I tell you to.” So perhaps we can start to approach that and share with our students more planful and intentional ways of getting to that point, where there’s a boundary, and the human is putting in the creativity, and the human makes the final call. Beck: We’re going to take a short break. When we come back: Does outsourcing our learning pose a threat to democracy? Beck: I’m really curious how you think about all this when it comes to civics and, not to put too fine a point on it, the continued existence of democracy. (Uneasy laughter.) Just because, you know, if people start to abandon learning or, maybe less extreme, abandon control over their own learning like you were saying, what might we lose or what might happen on the level of society and democracy? Miller: Oh my gosh. That is a really big challenge, and it means that when we go around and say, Well, our fellow citizens should just, you know, develop critical thinking, I, as an educator, go, “Okay, yes, they should, and oh my gosh, that’s hard.” Beck: And like critical thinking for what? Miller: Yes, critical thinking for what? And it’s simply hard. I sometimes say that critical thinking is like learning to walk on your hands, you know, or any kind of formal reasoning. We can do it if we’re motivated to do it. Nobody does it as a default. Nobody comes in and says, Oh, here’s something in my feed that says, you know, lemon juice will cure every disease I know, and I should just drink lemon juice. I think we’ve all seen those blog posts that go by. Beck: Yeah, the terrible ads at the bottom of articles. “One mom discovered the secret to anti-aging.” Miller: Yeah, discovered the secret to anti-aging. You need to think critically about that. But you need something on the order of six weeks, a whole semester, or more to really produce measurable gains. So we do not need to fear that the presence of AI is gonna wipe out critical thinking. In fact, though, we do need new critical-thinking skills. There’s even research on visual critical thinking, which all of a sudden we need to know, like, how do you look at a photograph and get some immediate red flags of: Is this AI-generated or not? Miller: It stresses us all out. So we need it, and this is the time for us to speak up and say, What are the ways that we can use our technology to develop critical-thinking skills, and how can we challenge ourselves to resist those biases that are at the center of a lot of critical thinking? So things like: Don’t just cherry-pick what you agree with or feels good on a gut level. Don’t just go with anecdotes. Be sure you understand correlation versus causation. All of those things are incredibly important, and technology can be a positive here, but only if we’re very, very deliberate about it. Beck: Mmm. Often when we talk about technology, right, we mean a pretty small slice of technology, like modern digital computing-based technologies. And as we were saying, like, knitting is technology; archery is technology. Many things are technology. They’re just not new. So I wonder, would expanding our minds about what technology actually is make a difference in how we think about using technology to learn, how we learn alongside technology? Do you think that would change anything? Miller: Well, I think yes, in the sense—especially in the sense if we talk about our relationship to it. And we sometimes do speak about it like something that came from outer space and was just sort of imposed on us. And we have to remind ourselves, technology, whether it is a knitting needle or it’s a social media or a chatbot, is something that, at least collectively, we human beings created because it did something that we very much wanted. And so it is part of us. And sometimes I, you know, instead of thinking of it as this external thing that showed up, think of it as a mirror. And I would also steer us away from, if I could, thinking of it as this environmental pollutant. And that’s hard because that is a very sort of clickbaity—we wanna see, like, Oh my gosh, exposure to technology does X, Y, and Z to kids, or is affecting my brain in some way. Miller: Yes, it’s almost like it’s like smoke or, you know, electrical waves from some place. But it’s not a pollutant. It’s not a carcinogen. I don’t think that that’s an appropriate metaphor. And when we talk about it that way, it does steer us into that binary, right? Like, should children be around cigarette smoke? No, they should not. Probably nobody should be around secondhand smoke. But with technology, it’s like, well, all right, but what was the type of use? What was the level of use? We see this all the time in research on technology. It’s not the hours on a smartphone, for example, that makes the big difference in cognitive or even mental-health factors. It’s: What were you doing when you were on it? So getting back to: What are you doing with it? Why are we doing it that way? Why did we even build a technology that could do this at such a high level, and why did people want it so badly? In my ideal world, that’s how we would talk about technology. Beck: I wanna go back to something you said about standing at the base of the mountain. You wanna become an expert in something. Even if you’re standing at just a little hill, right? And you don’t even wanna be an expert in something, but maybe you just wanna be okay at it. For instance, I’m trying to learn Korean right now. My in-laws are Korean. It’s hard. My brain is aging. I don’t retain vocabulary as well as I used to. I could just hold the phone up and have it translate for me, but I feel like that wouldn’t be the same. So I guess I’m curious, not just when you’re on a journey to becoming an expert in something, but when you’re just trying to learn anything to be a little bit better at it, or to be pretty good, is there still value in learning that thing that you could outsource or that you don’t have to learn? Miller: Languages, as always, they’re kind of their own special category, right? And the thing with spoken language: They’re real time. The substantial amount of time and energy. I mean, there’s always opportunity cost, especially in learning a language. I can think of nothing else where it’s like, if you’re gonna—it’s not gonna be a hill. It’s going to be a mountain, and you’re going to need to climb and climb and climb, and there’s a lot of things you’re gonna have to let go that you can’t learn and you can’t do if you choose that one. But that’s up to you, and you’ve got this clear picture of: Here’s where I’m gonna be on the other side. I am going to be able to speak in my own voice with no delay, with no weird barrier in between us, to these people who I love and care about. And that feeling too, you’re probably experiencing that, and I think this is common to lots of things that are difficult to learn. Barebow archery, I mean, when you hit that bullseye, wow, you feel it. And when you have the words coming out of your own mouth and your own brain? That feels really, really good. I mean, that’s your brain’s reward systems kicking in. Our reward systems don’t just kick in over, you know, sugar and doom scrolling. They kick in when we get it right, and that’s your opportunity to get it right. Beck: Yeah. That’s so true. And going back to the, like, building the brain you wanna live in, like you said, I mean, people will always say, and I find this to be true, that if you think in a different language, like you do think differently. You are almost a slightly different person in a different language, when you embody a different language, than in your native language. And so if you outsource that, then you maybe lose that opportunity to find out who you are in Korean, to find out who you are in French, or whatever it may be. Miller: Yes, and a plug for language learning as well. I mean, we haven’t quite gotten into this other highly practical aspect of learning, but time and again, we’ve seen that really substantial learning projects, like learning a language in particular, may offer a buffer against, you know, some of the typical changes that come with age and possibly against some age-related diseases and disorders. Now, that said, I always caution people: Don’t think of the brain as a muscle and you’re just gonna build it up. But when it comes to something like actually jumping in and learning Korean. I mean, you’re physically building whole new structures in your brain, and that can actually help. So we could see some real improvements if we did make even greater—if we did invest even more as a society in serious lifelong learning. Brennan: Julie, from what I am hearing from your decision to learn Korean is that a core part of the answer to the question that we posed at the beginning of this episode—why we learn things we don’t have to—is that to you, that intention matters right? It’s a way of showing your devotion. Beck: Yeah, I mean, a machine might be able to say what I want to say in grammatically perfect Korean. Or maybe not; I do think machines sometimes miss out on cultural nuances. But regardless, I have to imagine it means more to people hearing me try and make mistakes and make that effort to connect. Brennan: Right, like trying is the meaningful part. Beck: Yeah. You know, there’s this quote from the writer Oliver Burkeman that I really like. And he wrote, “It isn’t really the thought that counts, but the effort—which is to say the inconvenience. When you render the process more convenient, you drain it of its meaning.” Brennan: Yeah, like that feels like the point of learning to me. Learning is a way to engage more deeply, whether that’s engaging with a relationship, engaging with the world around you, or engaging with your own mind. Like Miller said, building the brain you want to live in. Whatever you’re learning, learning is a way to invest care. Beck: Yes, totally. And that really brings me back to the question of: What is the connection between learning and democracy? Or between learning and being a good citizen? And I think it’s that. Like, do you care enough to engage, or don’t you? Beck: That’s all for this episode of How to Touch Grass. Trump Will Make South Korea Pay for His Iran Humiliation
2026-08-17

Trump Will Make South Korea Pay for His Iran Humiliation

The Atlantic | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
Last winter, Donald Trump plunged recklessly into war with Iran. The United States is now losing that conflict. Iran remains defiant, much of the world economy is more threatened now than it was six months ago, U.S. military resources are overextended, and munition stores are dangerously depleted. Trump and the United States have suffered one embarrassment after another; last week, he had to sneak away from the region on a backup plane because of a possible assassination threat from Tehran. And so the president of the United States has taken the logical next step: He is punishing South Korea, a loyal American friend, and tempting U.S. enemies to doubt America’s will to defend its allies. Early Sunday evening, Trump instructed the Defense Department to “substantially reduce” its participation in an annual joint military exercise with the South Koreans. He did this, he said in a post on Truth Social, in part because such activities are expensive, but worse, they are insulting to his friends in North Korea, a country that has been “unthreatening and respectful” while he’s been in office. (Trump seems unconcerned about the dozens of nuclear weapons Pyongyang is pointing at U.S. and South Korean forces—many of them developed on his watch. Just a reminder: There are roughly 28,500 American troops currently stationed in South Korea, some just a few miles from the DMZ.) More to the point, he complained that the South Koreans have not helped him defeat Iran: While somewhat unrelated (?), I recently asked the President of South Korea if they would like to join us in the Denuclearization of the Islamic Republic of Iran, and they said, “No thanks!” When Trump makes the effort to say something is “unrelated” to his actions (and then adds a coy question mark) it usually means it is completely related—and probably his central motivation. Scaling back the exercise is, of course, an insane decision, which does nothing but hurt an ally, and tempt an adversary to think—correctly—that American leadership is feckless. You could be forgiven for wondering why South Korea and the rest of the free world must pay for Donald Trump’s failures. Such confusion is understandable, except that in Trump’s world, every loss, every defeat, is a personal insult. When things go wrong, someone must be held responsible—and that someone is never Donald Trump. America and the rest of the world are now witnessing a public exhibition of narcissistic rage. Trump embarked on a war for personal glory, and what he got instead was ongoing humiliation. He clearly finds it all unbearable. Trump both fears and respects the autocrats who rule Russia, China, and North Korea, and so he dares not cross them. Instead, he is seeking to assuage the psychic injury of his loss in Iran—including the American public learning of the shameful conditions to which he has subjected U.S. forces—by kicking America’s allies, the nations he can punish without fear that they will kick him back. The Iran war was not the president’s failure, you see. Others failed the president. Those disloyal South Koreans, who every few years drag him into a needless confrontation with that respectful and peace-loving young man in Pyongyang, refused to help him topple the Iranian regime. And because Trump is unable to subdue Iran, unable to replenish stocks of U.S. weapons he should never have expended in this war, unable to take care of the service personnel he has put in harm’s way, someone is going to pay. The most convenient target he could find, at least for the moment, was South Korea. At a better moment in American history, an enraged Congress would step in and demand answers about such dangerous idiocy. They would rush to the White House, talk to the press, hold hearings, and in general act like responsible stewards of American power. Those days, at least among legislators of the president’s party, are long past. They will take whatever Trump dishes out. Our allies might for the time being similarly ignore Trump’s tantrums, but they have power and agency of their own, and even now are likely planning for a world in which the Americans are no longer men and women of principle who stand by their word and by their friends. The Chinese and the Russians and the North Koreans are certain to draw the appropriate lessons from this deranged policy. They have been shown, yet again, that when Trump fails, he hurts those he feels safe hurting. Already, North Korea is dispatching missiles and thousands of soldiers to aid Russia in its war on Ukraine. If a conflict should break out in the Pacific or on the Korean peninsula, or if the war in Europe spreads farther west, the world’s dictators might well count on Trump to focus American wrath not on the enemies of the free world, but on the nations that have always pledged to stand side by side with the United States in a mutual defense of freedom.

Dear (Juris)Prudence: It Was One of the Biggest Scandals of the First Trump Term and It’s Even Worse Now. Where Did It Go?
2026-08-17

Dear (Juris)Prudence: It Was One of the Biggest Scandals of the First Trump Term and It’s Even Worse Now. Where Did It Go?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
Last week’s episode of Amicus included a mailbag special in which Dahlia Lithwick and Mark Joseph Stern answered listeners’ burning questions about the law. Amicus listeners have a lot of smart questions, so we’re running an occasional “Dear (Juris)Prudence” series in which we share your questions and Mark and Dahlia’s answers. Write to amicus@slate.com to pose a question to Dahlia and Mark. This transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. Dear (Juris)Prudence, I’m a retired lawyer and a longtime Plusketeer who loves your podcast. I keep wondering whether Trump’s crypto, airplane, and other grifts violate the Emoluments Clause of the Constitution. More generally, I am not sure what constitutes an emolument. I would love to hear your opinions on this. Dahlia Lithwick: I love this question because it reminds us how far we’ve come from the robust discussions we used to have in 2017 about emoluments, where that was the only game in town, and now it’s, in some ways, like the trailing toilet paper on the shoe, it’s almost so trivial compared to so many other things. Yet, it’s in the Constitution, not one place, but two places. And it’s still the law. When you start googling about the emoluments clause, the first thing that keeps coming up is “What ever happened to the emoluments clause,” as though it was excised from the Constitution. No, friends, there’s two emoluments clauses! They really are totally uncontroversial, and both clauses were seen as, like, these original anti-corruption, anti-grifting prohibitions, and were largely just observed. The foreign emoluments clause prevents the president and other federal officers from accepting “Any present, emolument, office or title of any kind whatever from any king, prince, or foreign state.” And it requires the consent of Congress to do so, and it’s introduced at the Constitutional Convention by Charles Pinckney of South Carolina. There’s a culture at the time of lavish gift-giving to diplomats and foreign officials, and he’s just terrified that officers of the United States will be beholden and influenced by foreigners. And the convention approves this unanimously without noted debate because it’s so freaking obvious, right? That you don’t want to be accepting fancy pens or, like, gorgeous gold earrings or an airplane, which wasn’t invented yet. So that’s the foreign emoluments clause. The domestic emoluments clause provides for the president to receive a fixed salary and bars him from receiving “Any other emolument from the United States or any of them.” Again, this is designed to keep the president from being beholden to or pressured by Congress or any individual state, right? They are both still in the document. And in addition to that, it’s very, very clear what it means. There’s not a lot of doctrine about this because, as I said, it was self-enforced by presidents historically, and the Justice Department is very clear the guidance provides that the clauses prohibit receipt of profits, advantages, or benefits from a foreign government without the consent of Congress, or from any U.S. state. None of this is in dispute, and here’s the bitch of it: The Constitution doesn’t have an enforcement mechanism, and because it was voluntarily complied with and presidents historically erred on the side of caution, we literally don’t have a magic red emoluments button to press when presidents start to violate it, and when presidents are close to the line, right? Barack Obama seeks formal legal guidance when he’s trying to decide whether he can accept the cash prize that comes along with his Nobel Peace Prize. But generally, presidents were very, very good about asking their lawyers, or as the foreign emoluments provision says, asking Congress. And I guess what’s so depressing about this question is that we learned that what we thought was another enforceable, immutable rule was just a convention and a norm, because when Trump first takes office in 2017, he wouldn’t separate himself from his business holdings, right? His hotels, which he wouldn’t separate himself from, were teeming with foreign actors who learned that they could buy influence there. And there were a bunch of lawsuits that were filed at the time and some really interesting rulings from lower courts around the country, and the whole bunch of it is dismissed in 2021 when the Supreme Court just kicks away two cases on procedural grounds. Why? Because Trump has left office. So, they had an opportunity to say, “Hey, future presidents who want to accept gifts and grifts, there’s some law here,” but they didn’t. Fine. But what it meant was that an emboldened Trump comes back into office and is like, Huh, there’s no law here. And he goes on to, as the question suggests, accept and construct horrifying grifts, right? We’ve got the World Liberty financial trading platform. We’ve got the memecoins. We’ve got this $400 million luxury plane, right? And this is all presumably OK. So I just want to end by noting that, of course, there’s a role for Congress here. We have been talking in the last couple of weeks about popular constitutionalism. We could lean on Congress and say: “Once upon a time in 1966, Congress established the statutory protection for handling gifts.” This was the Foreign Gifts and Decorations Act. Congress can do lots more. It could pass legislation right now that guarantees that there’s a right to sue so that we know who has standing under the emoluments clauses to clarify what counts as an emolument. There’s a lot of stuff here that Congress can do, and they’re not going to do it. Although I should note that Jamie Raskin just introduced two resolutions seeking enforcement mechanisms for the emoluments clauses. So I just want to note that this is sitting around on the table doing nothing. And it’s doing nothing in part because I think the American public conversation has moved on, in part because Congress is going to do nothing about it until and unless Congress changes, but in part because we the people failed to press Congress to say, “If we really think it’s hinky AF to get $400 million planes, there are mechanisms to do something about it.” And so I want to really end on this note of: Waiting around for someone else to do something about the enforcement of the emoluments clause hasn’t really worked thus far, and to the extent it might work, it’s going to work because we make them do something about it. Mark Joseph Stern: This idea of republican virtue, lowercase r, civic virtue, the Framers were obsessed with it. And so, you noted, they put this in the Constitution twice because they were deeply concerned about corruption, and they felt that corrupt leaders—and here corrupt presidents—would corrupt an entire nation and forever destroy the American Republic. So these are not just sort of weird random afterthoughts or vestiges. These are actually important provisions of the Constitution. For much of history, presidents adhered to them, including by seeking approval from Congress before accepting gifts from foreign states and foreign leaders. We actually had a system where presidents would follow the Constitution, go to Congress, say, “May I receive these?,” and Congress would take an up or down vote. Trump hasn’t even tried to do that, right? So he’s not even pretending to comply with these clauses, even though they’re vital anti-corruption protections in the Constitution. The courts have sort of bounced this. The Supreme Court said, Well, he’s out of office, so it doesn’t matter anymore. The cases against him in his first term about emoluments were sort of slow-walked, right? And the Supreme Court clearly didn’t want to touch them. Query why emoluments get the backhand, and this clause is sort of forgotten and maybe implicitly deemed as this backwater. As compared to something like the Second Amendment, which also doesn’t have a judicial enforcement mechanism, right? There’s certainly nothing that says, “The Supreme Court shall have the power to strike down laws that violate this amendment,” and also doesn’t actually guarantee an individual right by its plain text. Why is that a favored constitutional provision while this stuff all gets shoved to the side? Finally, I guess I’ll just say I think that the expansion of executive power is a huge part of what has allowed Trump to violate the emoluments clauses with such abandon, right? Because even when Congress tried to investigate this stuff in the first term, and there was a case—part of one of the sort of financial investigation cases at the Supreme Court was about the emoluments clause and Congress’ attempt to enforce it and pass legislation to enforce it. And the Supreme Court came in with this decision saying, Well, we need to protect executive power and the separation of powers and executive prerogative, and we’re worried about Congress entrenching on executive power. So we’re going to make it harder for Congress to investigate the president’s acceptance of emoluments. That is backwards from what the Framers would have wanted. And Trump’s ever-expanding ability to do whatever he wants and say, “Courts can’t review it. Congress can’t interfere with it,” citing the immunity decision, now citing Slaughter, citing all of this stuff that the Supreme Court has made up to give Trump unlimited power, he is able to kind of smuggle emoluments through those loopholes. So this is just another case of an imperial president constructed out of whole cloth by a Supreme Court that is not following the original meaning or plain text of the Constitution. Donald Trump Has Committed Too Many High Crimes to Count. But Should Democrats Really Impeach Again?
2026-08-17

Donald Trump Has Committed Too Many High Crimes to Count. But Should Democrats Really Impeach Again?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story each week about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back. You’ll also receive updates on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. If, as seems likely, Democrats win control of the House of Representatives in November, there will be tremendous pressure from the Democratic base to start a third round of impeachment proceedings against Donald Trump. If Democrats also win the Senate, the pressure will be still greater. Trump himself has predicted that if Democrats win the midterms, “I’ll get impeached.” Would another impeachment of Donald Trump make sense? Would it be constitutionally supportable, and would it be in the best interests of the country? I attempt to answer those questions in a new book—Great and Dangerous Offences: The Case For (and Against) Impeaching Trump a Third Time—due out in early 2027. In this end-of-summer series, “Try, Try Again?,” I will lay out the case for impeachment that is most likely to earn buy-in from American voters and also consider the best arguments against another Trump impeachment. Anyone considering a third Trump impeachment has to answer two questions: First, has Trump engaged in impeachable conduct in his second term? Second, even if so, what is the purpose of a third impeachment effort, and is that purpose achievable? The clear answer to the first question is yes. Indeed, the perverse difficulty confronting any Trump impeachment project is that he has committed so many impeachable offenses that categorizing and explaining them all in ways the public can easily digest is a daunting challenge. Unlike the British, from whom the America’s founders adapted impeachment, the Framers wrote a definition of impeachable conduct into the Constitution—“treason, bribery, or other high Crimes and Misdemeanors.” That definition has often fomented debate in prior presidential impeachments, primarily because the meaning of high Crimes and Misdemeanors is not immediately evident from the words themselves. Whatever else this enigmatic phrase may cover, it is plain from British constitutional history, the statements of those involved in drafting and ratifying the U.S. Constitution, and subsequent American practice that it reaches corruption in office for the personal gain of oneself or one’s family, serious criminality, and direct violations of significant provisions of the Constitution. More importantly, it covers an effort by the president to subvert the constitutional order by evading its structure of interbranch checks and balances, undercutting democracy, degrading the rule of law, and setting himself up as an elected autocrat. In his second term, Donald Trump has done all these things. His impeachable offenses include: In the first year after his second inauguration in January 2025, Trump is reported to have made a staggering $2.24 billion. Much of this money has come directly from abuses of his presidential office. He has filed lawsuits in his personal capacity against businesses subject to federal regulation or dependent on federal contracts and leveraged his control over the executive branch to extort settlements of tens of millions of dollars. He sued the IRS over leaks of his personal tax returns, then used his control over the Justice Department to secure a collusive “settlement” that, among other things, purports to give him and members of his family immunity from federal tax audits. If upheld, the immunity would eliminate a pending tax liability estimated at $100 million. This agreement may be a federal felony as well. Trump has also monetized public information by making his for-profit Truth Social media platform a primary venue for announcing government policy and, more egregiously, by launching Truth Social API, which sells early access to Trump’s announcements to institutions with financial interests in trading on such early access. Trump has reportedly made more than $1.4 billion from cryptocurrency investments, facilitated by gutting the civil and criminal regulatory structure around crypto, the award of pardons to convicted cryptocurrency businessmen (including Changpeng Zhao, whose Binance company helped set up the Trump family crypto business), and accepting money from foreign government–backed entities and dodgy private businessmen. The domestic and foreign emoluments clauses were inserted into the Constitution to prevent foreign powers, Congress, other federal executive officers, and state governments from buying influence with the president. Trump has violated both. He continues his first-term practice of taking in millions of dollars from state and federal officials who visit his resort properties on government or political business. He accepted a $400 million luxury airplane from Qatar to be used as Air Force One and, he claims, to be later transferred to his presidential library. The Trump Organization, which he controls, has entered into a series of staggeringly lucrative agreements with entities backed by foreign governments with significant interest in U.S. government policy (e.g., Qatar, Saudi Arabia, and Abu Dhabi). When the Framers spoke of impeachable corruption, they meant not merely corruption that enriched the president and his family, but also the kind of corruption endemic in monarchical Britain, where the king controlled politics through the award of titles, offices, commercial concessions, and outright bribes to members of Parliament and other powerful people. Donald Trump has embraced this sort of corruption, albeit in a contemporary form. In modern politics, money buys power. To secure it, Trump has used the stick of legal threats against businesses he dislikes, as he did with multiple national law firms. He has also offered carrots to businesses and the individual rich in the form of grants of lucrative government contracts, removal of antitrust barriers to mergers and acquisitions, direct government investment in favored businesses, destruction of civil and criminal enforcement mechanisms aimed at corporate crime, and pardons of business criminals who support him or are connected to those in his orbit. His reward has been a torrent of contributions to his political and personal projects. Perhaps the most fundamental authority of Congress is its “power of the purse”—the exclusive power conveyed by Article 1 of the Constitution to both impose taxes and control the expenditure of federal money. By repeatedly imposing tariffs in violation of law, he has usurped Congress’ power of taxation. By routinely dismantling federal programs and agencies, withholding congressionally appropriated funds, and unilaterally canceling congressionally authorized grants, all without congressional authority, he has usurped Congress’ power over federal expenditure. Moreover, he has selectively withheld funding to organizations, states, or regions associated with his real or perceived political opponents in violation of law and, arguably, of the First and Fifth amendments to the Constitution. Article 4, Section 3 of the Constitution confers on Congress the exclusive power to “dispose of and make all needful Rules and Regulations respecting the … property of the United States.” By tearing down the East Wing of the White House and commencing the $900 million construction of a ballroom on the site without congressional approval—using private funds but mostly taxpayer dollars—Trump violated the property clause and congressional appropriations authority. Trump has systematically violated the freedom of the press guaranteed by the First Amendment. He has filed meritless lawsuits against media companies and social media platforms and extorted large monetary settlements. Acting through his handpicked chair of the Federal Communications Commission, he has threatened the broadcast licenses of media outlets that provide unfavorable coverage of him. He has actively sought to suppress First Amendment–protected freedom of speech by characterizing political opponents as “terrorists” subject to criminal investigation and prosecution. He has discouraged the freedom of assembly by threatening to cut off federal funding to any college or university that “allows” demonstrations of which he disapproves. He has authorized repeated fatal missile and drone attacks on boats in the Caribbean Sea and eastern Pacific Ocean based on mere suspicion that their occupants were engaged in smuggling illegal drugs. To date, over 200 persons have been killed. These strikes violate both military and civilian law and amount to criminal homicide. Essential to both personal liberty and constitutional governance is the rule of law. Trump has effectively dismantled federal civil regulatory and criminal investigative and prosecutorial agencies as neutral enforcers of national law, transforming them into agents of favoritism for himself, his family, and his supporters, and instruments of retribution and suppression against his critics. He has reinstated in modern America a keystone of absolute monarchy by declaring his personal opinions about the law to be binding on all employees of the executive branch, including administrative law judges. He has repeatedly attacked and attempted to delegitimize Article 3 judges with whose opinions he disagrees. His anti-immigrant campaign has consistently and unconstitutionally denied due process of law to persons detained by federal officials. Trump has already been impeached once for scheming to overturn a presidential election. He compounded that offense on the first day of his second term by pardoning or commuting the sentences of his Jan. 6 criminal confederates and co-conspirators. Multiple Framers explicitly declared that a pardon of this sort would be an impeachable offense. When creating the presidency, one of the Framers’ greatest worries was that holders of the new office would break through all the anti-monarchical provisions of the Constitution to become autocrats. It was in part to this fear that George Mason referred when he insisted at the Constitutional Convention that any definition of impeachable conduct must cover “attempts to subvert the Constitution.” Taken as a whole, Trump’s conduct during his second term amounts to a wholesale attack on the constitutional order and, as British parliamentarians put it in several of their impeachment efforts, an attempt “to introduce an arbitrary and tyrannical way of government.” In any previous era of American history, this article could stop here. The president is openly corrupt. He is mounting a daily assault on American constitutionalism. He is guilty of a cornucopia of impeachable high crimes and misdemeanors. Therefore, impeachment in the House should commence. Conviction and removal in the Senate would surely follow. The problem, of course, is that the last statement is not true. The Constitution requires a two-thirds vote in the Senate—67 senators—to convict in an impeachment proceeding. Even if the Democrats were to win control of the Senate in the midterms, gaining a 51–49 majority, conviction would require the votes of every Democrat and 16 Republican senators. The odds of such a mass Republican defection from Trump’s cult of personality are close to zero. If one doubts the point, remember that in 2021, only weeks after Trump incited an attack on the Capitol—and on the members of Congress themselves—in an effort to overturn an election, a mere seven Republican senators, five of whom are no longer in Congress, voted for his impeachment. Consider also the recent Senate confirmation of Todd Blanche as attorney general. Blanche has been Trump’s agent in committing multiple impeachable offenses. He has overseen the conversion of the Department of Justice from neutral law-enforcement agency to instrument of presidential spite and retribution. He has helped dismantle the legal structures that might have restrained the Trump family’s crypto corruption. He was the primary architect of the corrupt, collusive, and illegal “settlement” of Trump’s IRS suit. Yet, knowing all that, 50 of 53 Republican senators voted to install Blanche as attorney general (Mitch McConnell being absent). Can it seriously be imagined that any more would vote to convict and remove Trump for being the author and beneficiary of Blanche’s misdeeds? If a third impeachment is attempted, it must proceed with the clear-eyed recognition that, regardless of the charges or the strength of the evidence, Republican senators will almost certainly not abandon Trump in sufficient numbers to convict him. One answer might be that the House has a duty to commence impeachment proceedings if a president has engaged in impeachable conduct, regardless of the likely outcome of the case in the Senate. I certainly agree that the mindless loyalty of a president’s party should not automatically bar the House from investigating presidential misconduct or considering articles of impeachment if there is ample evidence to support them. But it must always be remembered that impeachment is a political remedy for the political problem of an unfit, corrupt, or autocratic president. There is no constitutional duty to unlimber the remedy if it will not solve, or at least help solve, the problem. In Trump’s case, impeachment will almost certainly not expel him from office. It should therefore be started only if it can serve some other beneficial public end. The biggest potential benefits of an unsuccessful impeachment flow from the investigation itself. Congress has the authority to investigate presidential actions using its general oversight power. However, the power to compel compliance with congressional information demands is universally agreed to be greater in an impeachment inquiry. Although recent history suggests that Trump will defy Congress however strong its constitutional position, any investigation of Trump should be armed with all the legal authority it can muster. Moreover, a formal impeachment investigation provides a high-profile public forum in which to educate the public about presidential wrongdoing. To be sure, all of the Trump misdeeds mentioned above (and more) need to be investigated, publicized, and sharply critiqued by a Democratic-majority Congress. The conduct, considered piece by piece and in the aggregate, is so outrageous that focusing public attention on it ought to increase votes against the Trump GOP’s autocratic turn and toward a return to democracy. The optimistic view that congressional investigations will move public opinion has to be weighed against the real possibility that framing Trump’s many transgressions in terms of impeachment risks diminishing their impact on the voting public. The sad truth is that impeachment has in recent years been so often employed by each party against the officials of the other that many voters are quite likely to discount, even scoff at, any impeachment campaign as a crassly partisan gesture. And if that reaction is common, impeachment may only hobble the transcendently important project of defeating Trumpism and restoring healthy constitutional government. I am certain that Trump is a dangerous would-be autocrat who has committed a plethora of high crimes and misdemeanors. At the same time, I am not yet certain whether a hoped-for Democratic majority in the House should launch a formal impeachment proceeding against him. Still, laying out the major grounds on which Trump is impeachable may convince voters to reject candidates who persist in supporting Trump’s ongoing assault on constitutional democracy. With or without a third impeachment, Trumpism can only be finally defeated if the American electorate rejects it. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
