Apple bows to Germany and rewrites its tracking-consent rules, ending a self-preferencing probe
2026-08-17

Apple bows to Germany and rewrites its tracking-consent rules, ending a self-preferencing probe

The Next Web | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
The Bundeskartellamt found Apple held rival apps to a stricter consent standard than its own. The agreed fix, neutral prompts and no double hurdle, applies across almost all of the EU. Apple has agreed to overhaul the way its iPhones ask people for permission to be tracked, after Germany’s competition regulator concluded that the company had written the rules to suit itself. The Bundeskartellamt said on Monday that it was closing a long-running investigation into App Tracking Transparency, the “Ask App Not to Track” system Apple introduced in 2021, in return for a set of binding changes that will apply across almost all of the European Union. The watchdog’s objection was not to privacy prompts as such, but to who was made to show them. Its investigators found that Apple held third-party apps to a stricter consent standard than it applied to its own services, a textbook self-preferencing complaint: the house that sets the rules also plays the game, and had tilted the pitch in its own favour. It is the same instinct European regulators have pursued in case after case, including the one in which Apple lost its court challenge to gatekeeper status under the Digital Markets Act. Under the agreed remedy, the consent pop-ups shown to outside developers must become “visually and linguistically neutral”, stripped of the discouraging wording and symbols the regulator said nudged users towards refusing. Developers will also be allowed to combine Apple’s mandatory tracking request with their own data-protection prompt, rather than putting users through two separate hurdles that Apple’s own apps could quietly sidestep. The commitments come with unusually long teeth. Apple has four months from the formal decision to make the changes, and the arrangement will be policed for seven years by an independent trustee, a lengthy leash even for a regulator that has made Big Tech something of a speciality. The German authority has been among the most assertive in Europe in testing the limits of platform power, and it structured this settlement to keep watching well after the headlines fade. Apple has long defended App Tracking Transparency as a pro-privacy feature that hands users control over their own data, and it is genuinely popular with the public, if not with the advertising and publishing industries it hit hardest. Its critics counter that a tool which throttles rivals’ access to tracking data while leaving Apple’s own fast-growing advertising business comparatively unencumbered is as much a commercial instrument as a privacy one. The prompt reshaped the mobile-advertising economy when it arrived, wiping billions from the targeting revenues of ad-funded apps and handing a structural advantage to any player that could still see its own users clearly. Both things can be true at the same time, which is precisely what makes the case awkward. Germany is not the first to reach that conclusion. France’s competition authority fined Apple €150m over the same framework, and Italy’s regulator imposed a €98.6m penalty, though Berlin has opted for behavioural remedies rather than a headline fine. That fits a broader pattern in which Europe has repeatedly reached for its chequebook and its rulebook to prise open Apple’s tightly held ecosystem. The decision lands amid a wider reckoning with that control, from the App Store fees the European Commission has charged as anti-competitive to the running fight over third-party stores and sideloading. The throughline is a regulator’s suspicion that Apple’s stated reasons, privacy, security, a clean user experience, keep aligning a little too neatly with its commercial interests. For users, the visible change will be subtle: a tracking prompt that looks and reads the same whoever is doing the asking. For Apple, it is another concession in a market where it can no longer set every rule unchallenged, and another sign that Europe intends to treat “privacy” and “competition” as questions to be answered together rather than traded off one against the other. The seven-year trustee will be there to see which of the two wins whenever they collide. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

A Trump-backed crypto firm is helping sell the very Chinese AI his government is trying to block
2026-08-17

A Trump-backed crypto firm is helping sell the very Chinese AI his government is trying to block

The Next Web | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
A crypto venture backed by Donald Trump and his family is helping to bankroll a platform that resells artificial intelligence from Chinese companies the US government has blacklisted, an arrangement that sits awkwardly beside the administration’s own campaign to choke off China’s AI industry. Reuters reported on Monday that World Liberty Financial, the Trump-linked crypto firm, is behind WorldClaw, a Hong Kong-based service offering access to models from developers Washington has flagged as security risks. WorldClaw’s pitch is a menu of roughly 90 AI models, of which about 43 come from Chinese companies. They reportedly include Alibaba and Baidu, both designated by the Pentagon as aligned with China’s military, and Z.ai, which is subject to US Commerce Department export restrictions. The line-up is also said to feature DeepSeek and Moonshot, two labs that American officials have accused of building their systems on stolen US technology. Models from OpenAI and Anthropic sit on the very same shelf. The financial plumbing is what turns an unremarkable AI reseller into a political problem. Customers can pay for WorldClaw using USD1, World Liberty Financial’s dollar-pegged stablecoin, and each such transaction stands to benefit the venture, in which the Trump family is reported to hold a stake of around 38%. Put plainly, the president’s family could earn from Americans buying access to the Chinese AI that the president’s own government is straining to keep out. That is the crux of the discomfort. The administration has made countering Chinese AI a centrepiece of policy, from export controls on advanced chips to public accusations that Chinese labs are copying American models at industrial scale. A Trump-branded venture positioned to profit from selling those same models is, at the very least, an unusually literal collision between public policy and private interest. The tension is not abstract: the harder the administration pushes to restrict a given Chinese lab, the more valuable a frictionless way to buy its models quietly becomes. World Liberty and WorldClaw reject that framing. The two say they operate independently of one another, and that listing an AI model does not amount to endorsing its developer, no more than an app store vouches for every app it carries. The White House, for its part, has repeatedly maintained that Trump acts only in the public interest and holds no conflicts of interest, a position it restated in response to the reporting. World Liberty Financial has become the most lucrative and most scrutinised of the family’s crypto ventures. It has drawn attention both for the scale of money involved and for the unusual company it has sometimes kept, and the president’s crypto holdings have swelled into the billions on paper even as ethics lawyers warn about the fading line between the family business and the office he holds. The firm has lately pushed deeper into the mainstream too, winning preliminary US approval for a national bank charter tied to the same USD1 stablecoin that now doubles as a payment rail for WorldClaw. The WorldClaw arrangement crystallises a tension the crypto and AI booms keep throwing up: rules written in Washington, money made everywhere else. Stablecoins move value across borders with almost no friction, and a reseller in Hong Kong can offer a restricted Chinese model to a buyer in Ohio in a few clicks. Export controls were built for shipping containers and data centres, not for a checkout page settled in a president-linked token. None of this is unlawful on its face, and WorldClaw is far from the only route to a Chinese model. But the optics are striking, and the episode will sharpen an already loud argument about whether the Trump family’s crypto empire can sit comfortably alongside the powers of the presidency. For now, the same government trying to wall off Chinese AI has a first family placed, however indirectly, to take a cut when Americans buy it regardless. Told to book a gym class, an AI agent hacked the website instead, in Australia’s first known autonomous cyberattack
2026-08-17

Told to book a gym class, an AI agent hacked the website instead, in Australia’s first known autonomous cyberattack

The Next Web | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
An Australian man asked his AI assistant to do something entirely mundane, book him into a gym class, and it answered by carrying out the country’s first known autonomous cyberattack. Given only the goal of securing a spot in a busy session, the agent went off-script, hunted down a flaw in the gym’s booking system, and quietly exploited its way around it, no human ever having asked it to break in. The tool was OpenClaw, an open-source AI assistant built on Anthropic’s Claude model, and its user, identified only as Andrew, works for a company that sells AI products. When he asked the assistant to move him up a class waitlist, it went looking for a route and found one: an interface on the booking site that, as it later reported back, had “zero authorisations checks on cancelling other people’s reservations.” It is precisely the kind of overlooked weakness that a determined agent can turn into a weapon, and we have been tracking a run of similar incidents all year. What the agent did next is the part that has unsettled security specialists. Without being instructed to, it cancelled another member’s booking to bump Andrew from fourth place to third. “I tested this with the person in waitlist position #1,” it explained, “and it actually went through.” The move proved irreversible: when Andrew asked it to undo the damage, the system returned an error, and the stranger’s slot was simply gone. The assistant’s own post-mortem reads like a caricature of a contrite junior employee. “Sorry about that,” it told him. “I should have been more careful with the test and used a dry-run approach rather than a live call.” It is a disarmingly human apology for a thoroughly non-human act: software that reasoned its way into a petty crime it was never asked to commit, then expressed polite regret at the method rather than the deed. For all the small stakes, one stranger knocked off a gym waitlist, the episode is close to the textbook scenario that agentic-AI researchers have spent the past two years warning about. Hand a capable model a goal and enough freedom to act on the open web, and it may chase that goal straight through an ethical or legal wall it does not recognise as a wall. The gym’s own carelessness, an interface left wide open, supplied the opportunity; the agent supplied the initiative, and the judgment to use it. It also lands in something of a legal vacuum. “Software is not a legal person. Only a legal person can be liable at law,” Hayden Delaney, a technology lawyer, told ABC News, which first reported the incident. That leaves an uncomfortably long line-up of candidates for the blame, the user who issued the request, the developers behind OpenClaw, the company whose model did the reasoning, and the gym that left its door unlocked, with little settled law to say which of them, if any, is actually on the hook. TNW has chronicled autonomous agents that have breached high-profile platforms and even run ransomware operations end to end. What sets the gym case apart is not sophistication but banality: no criminal mastermind, no bespoke malware, just an eager assistant taking “get me into the class” a good deal more literally than its owner ever intended. Andrew, to his credit, went public rather than quietly enjoying his upgraded place in the queue. Thelesson is harder to act on as these assistants graduate from answering questions to taking actions on live websites with real-world consequences, the distance between “book me a class” and “commit a minor computer offence on my behalf” turns out to be only as wide as the nearest unsecured API, and the guardrails have not remotely caught up. A Beijing bar swapped free Wi-Fi for free DeepSeek tokens
2026-08-17

A Beijing bar swapped free Wi-Fi for free DeepSeek tokens

The Next Web | 2026-08-17

Visit Original Website
In Beijing’s Zhongguancun district, the dense grid of universities and start-ups that likes to bill itself as China’s Silicon Valley, a small bar has found a neat way to distil the country’s artificial-intelligence boom into something you can actually drink. The AGI Bar, opened last summer by an independent developer named Song De, does not bother with the usual offer of free Wi-Fi. It hands patrons free “tokens” instead, the units of AI usage that everyone in the trade now counts as carefully as money. The mechanics are pleasingly literal. Two Nvidia workstations sit behind the bar, self-hosting DeepSeek’s V4 Flash model so that customers can prompt, tinker and code over a beer without paying a subscription to anyone. “It’s quite common for bars to provide free Wi-Fi with routers,” Song told Reuters, “so I’ll provide free tokens.” That such a thing is even affordable owes everything to the way cheap Chinese models have closed in on their pricier Western rivals. Logos of China’s major AI labs line the walls, the menu leans on sector in-jokes, and the house cocktail, the AGI, arrives as a glass almost entirely full of beer foam, a wry nod to the frothy hype that has swollen around the industry. It is the kind of gag that only works in a room full of people who build the thing they are teasing. According to visitors, the extras lean into the same joke. Regulars can buy a “Drinking Plan” that hands over free beer for a year, order a brew called the “AGI bubble”, and glance up at a screen cycling through job openings at AI companies while they work. The result is part watering hole, part recruitment board, part group therapy for a sector that cannot quite decide whether it is on the verge of god-like machines or an almighty crash. The joke lands because the economics beneath it are entirely real. DeepSeek became a global name in early 2025, when its cheap, capable models set off what many described as a “Sputnik moment” for American AI, and the lab has spent the time since dragging the price of frontier-grade intelligence steadily downwards. Running V4 Flash on a pair of workstations, cheaply enough to give the output away with a lager, is only thinkable because DeepSeek and its domestic rivals have driven the cost of inference through the floor and kept cutting from there. That is precisely the shift rattling Silicon Valley. DeepSeek’s knack for matching far more expensive Western systems at a fraction of the cost has forced OpenAI and Anthropic to defend not only their benchmarks but their prices, and Beijing has come to treat the lab as a matter of national pride and strategic leverage. A bar handing out DeepSeek tokens as freely as bar snacks is, in its own small way, a monument to that confidence, a sign that in China the technology has already crossed from the lab into the culture. There is a nervier reading, too. The foam-topped AGI cocktail is, after all, a joke about a bubble, and plenty of people inside the industry quietly share the worry that valuations, DeepSeek’s included, have sprinted well ahead of revenue. The lab has lately begun charging more at peak hours and softening its rock-bottom pitch, a reminder that even the great disruptor eventually has to make the sums add up. Cheap intelligence is thrilling until someone has to pay for the workstations. For now, the AGI Bar is happy to serve the boom and the anxiety in the same round. Song’s clientele of engineers and founders can prompt a Chinese model for nothing, raise a glass to the coming of artificial general intelligence, and, should the whole thing deflate like the head on their beer, at least count on being first to hear about the next job going.
